--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (score -1.66) is the only machine that actually scores negatively (good). All others score 0.00 or positive. The champion's structure:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(cyan/6) → transitions to state 0 when neighbors > 8 (high threshold)
- State 1: GoToColor(yellow/4) → 4 transitions: to state 2 when neighbors>6, to state 1 when BLACK floor, to state 0 when BLACK floor, to state 0 when GRAY floor
- State 2: Attraction → transitions to state 0 on WHITE floor, to state 1 on GRAY floor
- State 3: Attraction → transitions to state 3 on neighbors>5, to state 1 randomly

The key insight: GoToColor(yellow=4) likely goes toward yellow LEDs (other robots). GoAwayColor(cyan=6) avoids cyan. The machine navigates between source and nest.

For foraging: robots should go to source (black floor), pick up item from the GOOD half (blue wall), and return to nest (white floor). The scoring means one color of item is good (-1) and one is bad (+1). The champion seems to use GoToColor yellow (robots' own LEDs) for coordination.

**Strategy:**
1. Copy champion exactly (elitism)
2. Small variations of champion topology
3. More directed foraging machines: GoToColor(blue=3) for source, GoAwayColor(red=1) to avoid nest wall when carrying bad item, etc.
4. Machines that explicitly go to black floor (source) then white floor (nest)

Let me design machines:

**Exploitation (small edits of champion):**
- Vary probabilities/thresholds slightly
- Try different color targets

**Exploration:**
- Direct foraging: explore → go to black → go to white (nest)
- Use GoToColor(blue) to target blue half of source specifically
- Use GoAwayColor(red) to move away from red wall (nest side) when at source

FSM designs:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion variant: change att gains slightly
3. Champion variant: change probabilities
4. GoToColor(blue) + return to nest via GoAwayColor(red)
5. Exploration + GoToColor(blue) + GoToColor(white direction via floor sensor)
6. 3-state: explore, go-to-black, go-to-white
7. 4-state foraging focused on blue source
8. Different champion topology variant
9. Champion with different neighbor thresholds
10. Simpler 2-state GoToColor machine
11. 3-state with GoToColor blue and floor-based transitions
12. 4-state with explicit blue targeting
13. Exploration variant
14. Another topology

## Reasoning

Key observations from rounds 1-2:
- Champion (-1.66): GoAwayColor(cyan) → GoToColor(yellow) → Attraction → Attraction
- Round 2 best (-1.51): Similar structure but with extra WHITE-floor transition from state 0, and state 3 is Exploration instead of Attraction
- Round 2 #2 (-1.19): 3-state: GoAwayColor(cyan) + GoToColor(yellow) + Attraction - simpler but effective
- Round 2 #3 (-0.88): GoToColor(blue/3) → GoAwayColor(red/1) → Exploration → Attraction - the blue/red approach works!
- Score -3.25 for the GoToColor(blue)→GoAwayColor(red) 2-state machine (but it scored +3.25, the worst!)

Key insight: The champion uses GoAwayColor(cyan=6) and GoToColor(yellow=4). The LEDs of robots are cyan (state 0) and yellow (state 1). So robots doing GoAwayColor(cyan) spread out, and GoToColor(yellow) follows yellow-LED robots toward the source. This is indirect navigation via robot signaling.

The blue/red approach (GoToColor blue=3 source wall, GoAwayColor red=1 nest wall) works moderately but the 2-state version badly failed.

**Strategy for round 3:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Exploit champion topology with small tweaks
3. Exploit round 2 #1 (variant of champion with extra transition)
4. Exploit round 2 #2 (3-state version)
5. Exploit round 2 #3 (blue/red navigation)
6. Explore: combine best elements - GoAwayColor(cyan) spreading + blue source targeting + white floor return
7. Try GoToColor(blue) + GoAwayColor(red) with proper 3-4 state structure

The worst machine (score +3.25) was GoToColor(blue)→GoAwayColor(red) 2-state with self-looping transitions - this makes robots go to blue half (bad items?) then flee red wall, delivering bad items. So blue=bad, green=good? Or the architecture was just wrong.

Actually - GoToColor(blue=3) goes to the BLUE wall half. One half gives +1 (bad) and one gives -1 (good). The 2-state blue machine scored +3.25, meaning it delivered bad items. So BLUE wall = bad items, GREEN wall = good items. We need GoToColor(green=2)!

Let me redesign: GoToColor(green=2) to get good items, then GoAwayColor(red=1) or GoToColor(white floor) to return to nest.

## Reasoning

The new champion (-2.20) uses a very clear structure:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue=3) → on BLACK floor (p=0.9) go to state 1; if perceive color 2 (green) go to state 2
- State 1: GoAwayColor(red=1) → on WHITE floor (p=0.9) go to state 2; on BLACK floor go to state 0; on GRAY go to state 2
- State 2: Exploration → on BLACK floor go to state 0; on WHITE floor go to state 1
- State 3: Attraction → unused? (no transitions point to it from other states... wait)

Actually looking more carefully:
- S0: GoToColor(blue/3), LED yellow. Transitions: to S1 on BLACK(p=0.9), to S2 on perceive-color-2(green, p=0.5)
- S1: GoAwayColor(red/1), LED cyan. Transitions: to S2 on WHITE(p=0.9), to S0 on BLACK(p=0.3), to S2 on GRAY(p=0.2)
- S2: Exploration, LED magenta. Transitions: to S0 on BLACK(p=0.6), to S1 on WHITE(p=0.5)
- S3: Attraction, LED yellow. Transitions: to S0 on BLACK(p=0.7), to S1 on WHITE(p=0.6)

The logic: GoToColor(blue) navigates toward blue wall (source area). On BLACK floor (source), switch to GoAwayColor(red) to flee red wall (= go toward nest). On WHITE floor (nest), switch to Exploration. State 3 is reachable from... nowhere. It's essentially dead.

Key insight: GoToColor(blue=3) goes to blue source half. GoAwayColor(red=1) flees red wall = moves toward opposite end = nest delivery. This is the core foraging loop!

But wait - the two worst machines (scores +1.46, +2.44) also used GoToColor(green=2) and GoAwayColor(red=1). The difference: those machines transitioned on GRAY floor condition while champion uses BLACK floor condition. Also champion uses GoToColor(blue) not green.

So BLUE wall = good items (-1 score). GREEN wall = bad items (+1 score). The champion targets BLUE.

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Vary probabilities around champion's values
3. Add transition from state 3 to make it reachable
4. Try similar topology with tweaked floor transition probabilities
5. Try 3-state version (remove unused state 3)

**Exploration:**
- Try GoToColor(blue) + GoToColor(white) for return (instead of GoAwayColor(red))
- Add neighbor-based transitions
- Mix champion structure with LED-following from previous best

## Reasoning

Strong convergence is happening around a clear topology. Let me analyze the top performers:

**Champion (-3.31):** GoToColor(blue/3) → on BLACK go to S1(p=0.88), on perceive-green(2) go to S2(p=0.45)
S1: GoAwayColor(red/1) → on WHITE go to S2(p=0.88), on BLACK go to S0(p=0.32), on GRAY go to S2(p=0.22)
S2: Exploration(rwm=20) → on BLACK go to S0(p=0.62), on WHITE go to S1(p=0.52)
S3: Attraction (unreachable, dead state)

**Key observations:**
- The core loop is: GoToColor(blue) → [BLACK floor] → GoAwayColor(red) → [WHITE floor] → Exploration → [BLACK floor] → GoToColor(blue)
- The "perceive green" transition from S0 to S2 helps avoid the bad (green) half
- Probabilities around 0.88-0.92 for floor transitions work well
- rwm=20 for exploration outperforms rwm=25 or 30
- State 3 (Attraction) is unreachable but doesn't hurt
- Adding extra transitions (3-state variants, extra neighbor conditions) hurts performance
- GoToColor(blue) in S1 instead of GoAwayColor(red) also works (-2.76)

**What to try:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Slight variations of champion probabilities (0.85, 0.86, 0.87, 0.89, 0.90)
3. Try rwm=15 for exploration
4. Try making state 3 reachable as a helper
5. Try different att values for state 3
6. Add a 4th transition to state 1 (perceive blue to reinforce going to S0)
7. Try GoToColor(blue) for S1 with champion's other params
8. Explore: change exploration rwm, try different att gain for S3

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion score is stuck at -3.77 for two rounds now. The entire population is converging very tightly around this local optimum. Key observations:

**What's clearly working:**
- p0x0 = 0.86 (BLACK floor → S1): champion value
- p0x1 = 0.42 (perceive-green → S2): champion value  
- p1x0 = 0.86 (WHITE → S2): symmetric with p0x0
- p1x1 = 0.30 (BLACK → S0): champion value
- p1x2 = 0.20 (GRAY → S2): champion value
- rwm2 = 15: champion (rank 1), though 17 also works
- p2x0 = 0.70: champion
- p2x1 = 0.60: champion

**Diminishing returns:** We've explored p0x0 in [0.80-0.92], rwm in [13-22], p2x0 in [0.68-0.78]. The landscape is very flat with this topology.

**Strategies for round 8:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Try very fine-grained tweaks: rwm=14, 10, 12; p0x0=0.86±0.01
3. **Wider exploration needed** - try genuinely different parameters:
   - Try p0x1 lower (0.30-0.35) - less green avoidance
   - Try p2x0=0.80+ (more aggressive return to source)
   - Try combining rwm=15 with p2x0=0.75, p2x1=0.65 (rank 2's exploration values with champion's rwm)
   - Try different att values (1.0, 3.0) for dead state 3
   - Try adding a 4th transition to champion topology at S1 targeting blue perception
   - Try making S3 reachable to help stuck robots

The key insight: rwm=15 beat rwm=17 for the champion. Try even lower rwm (10-13) and different p2x values.

## Reasoning

Excellent progress! Champion jumped from -4.10 to -5.17 with rwm=5! The trend is clear: lower rwm = better. The ranking shows:
- rwm=5: -5.17 (champion)
- rwm=7: -4.42
- rwm=8: -4.62 (rank 2), -4.42 (rank 4 with p1x1=0.26)
- rwm=9: -3.95
- rwm=10: -3.85 to -4.22

So rwm=5 >> rwm=7,8 >> rwm=9,10. The minimum is rwm=1 (the lower limit). Let's try rwm=1, 2, 3, 4 to see if even lower helps.

**Key parameter analysis:**
- rwm2=5 with p0x0=0.86, p0x1=0.42, p1x1=0.30, p1x2=0.20, p2x0=0.70, p2x1=0.60 is the champion
- p1x1=0.26 with rwm=8 scored -4.42 (same as rwm=7 with p1x1=0.30) - the lower p1x1 helps
- p2x0=0.68 with rwm=10 scored -4.22 (better than rwm=10 standard) - interesting

**Strategy for final round:**
1. Champion (exact copy) - elitism
2. Try rwm=1, 2, 3, 4 - systematic search below 5
3. Combine rwm=5 with p1x1=0.26 (lower BLACK→S0 from S1)
4. Combine rwm=5 with p1x1=0.22 
5. Combine rwm=5 with p2x0=0.65 (slightly lower)
6. Combine rwm=3 with champion's other params
7. Try rwm=5 with p0x0=0.84 (slightly lower)
8. Try rwm=5 with p1x2=0.15 (lower GRAY)

This is the final round - maximize exploitation around the champion while probing rwm=1-4.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
